Chapter 10. Chapter 9: Policy and Governance in Practice

Which national and regional AI-in-education regulations currently exist, and how do their approaches compare?

## AI in Education: The Global Regulatory Landscape

The regulation of artificial intelligence in educational settings is now one of the most active — and most contested — areas of technology policy worldwide. Three distinct regulatory philosophies have emerged, each reflecting deeper assumptions about the proper relationship between states, institutions, and technology.

---

### The European Union: Rights-Based, Risk-Stratified Law

The most architecturally ambitious framework is the EU AI Act, the world's first comprehensive legal framework governing AI development and deployment, which classifies AI systems by risk level — from minimal to unacceptable — and assigns corresponding compliance obligations. For the education sector, this classification has direct consequences. Education sits at the intersection of two areas the Act treats with particular care: high-risk AI applications and the protection of vulnerable populations. Many common school applications — from automated essay grading and adaptive learning platforms to dropout prediction tools — fall squarely into the high-risk category under Annex III, covering AI systems that determine access, assess learning outcomes, or influence the educational path of individuals.

The prohibitions are explicit and already enforceable. Among eight banned practices, emotion recognition in educational institutions and real-time remote biometric identification are flatly prohibited; those prohibitions became effective in February 2025. Any system designed to infer student emotions from facial, vocal, or biometric cues is classified as "unacceptable risk" and is banned in EU educational settings.

The timeline has recently shifted, however. A legislative simplification package — the so-called "AI omnibus" — was adopted in November 2025, with a political agreement reached in May 2026. Under that agreement, the full compliance rules for high-risk AI systems in education will now apply from December 2027. Penalties for non-compliance are substantial: placing a non-compliant high-risk AI system on the market can result in fines of up to 15 million euros or 3 percent of global annual turnover, whichever is higher.

Alongside the binding Act, the European Commission has issued ethical guidelines for educators and the guidelines include clear explanations of the AI Act, the General Data Protection Regulation, and the principles of responsible AI use. The European Schools system, notably, has been candid that no generative AI tools have yet been officially approved for system-wide use, with approval procedures still under development.

---

### The United States: Federalism Without a Federal Floor

Across the Atlantic, the picture is one of deliberate decentralization. There is no comprehensive federal AI-in-education statute. The existing federal scaffolding rests on older laws: FERPA protects the privacy of student education records, but its last regulatory updates predate the widespread use of technology in learning environments, leaving institutions to interpret it in light of artificial intelligence. There are few federal regulations that govern AI directly at the higher education level.

The Trump administration's April 2025 executive order on AI education frames the issue almost entirely in competitiveness terms. It is the policy of the United States to promote AI literacy and proficiency among Americans by promoting the appropriate integration of AI into education, providing comprehensive AI training for educators, and fostering early exposure to AI concepts to develop an AI-ready workforce. The Department of Education subsequently issued grant guidance outlining how AI may be used across key educational functions and articulating principles for responsible use, affirming such uses are allowable under existing federal education programs.

With the federal level providing only encouragement and grant incentives, states have become the primary regulatory arena. When ChatGPT debuted in 2022, no states had policies related to generative AI; by April 2025, at least 28 states had engaged with the issue legislatively. The legislative priorities reveal a pragmatic rather than rights-based orientation. Fifteen bills in the 2025 session alone focused on promoting AI literacy for students and professional development for teachers, making this the top legislative priority. Several states have moved toward guardrails on specific harms: Nevada enacted legislation prohibiting public schools from using AI-powered tools to perform the functions of a school counselor, psychologist, or social worker in relation to student mental health. Vendor accountability is a newer frontier: legislators are increasingly mandating that AI EdTech vendors submit documentation about how they handle and use student data to train their products.

State approaches vary widely in ambition. States like California, Connecticut, and Texas have introduced bills to create oversight boards and "regulatory sandboxes" where AI tools can be tested before broader rollout. Tennessee, by contrast, passed a law mandating that all K-12 districts, charter schools, and universities adopt their own AI policies, with the Tennessee School Boards Association subsequently developing a model policy that most districts adopted in full.

---

### China: State-Directed Integration with Embedded Guardrails

China's approach is neither rights-centered like Europe's nor market-permissive like the United States'. It is best understood as state-directed deployment, combining ambitious top-down mandates with granular behavioral rules. China aims to promote AI in aiding education reform, with a joint guideline from the Ministry of Education and eight other departments emphasizing building an AI-based education system that integrates smart technologies into teaching, learning, assessment, and academic research.

The Ministry of Education issued two guidelines in May 2025: one for AI general education in primary and secondary schools, and one for the use of generative AI — prohibiting students from independently using open-ended content generation at the primary level and banning teachers from using generative AI as a substitute for their core teaching responsibilities. The curriculum is structured as a developmental ladder: primary school students encounter basic technologies like voice recognition and image classification; junior high students examine machine learning processes and develop critical thinking about misinformation in AI outputs; senior secondary students design and refine AI algorithm models while cultivating interdisciplinary systems thinking.

Several regions — including Beijing, Guangzhou, and Chengdu — have rolled out implementation plans specifying class hour requirements at each school stage and incorporating AI course evaluations into students' overall assessments; Beijing's primary and secondary schools will fully implement general AI education starting in fall 2025, with no fewer than eight class hours per academic year.

At the university level, academic integrity has become a pressure point. At Tianjin University of Science and Technology, AI-generated content in undergraduate theses must not exceed 40 percent under detection measures introduced in 2024. Fudan University's regulations ban AI use in six areas of academic work, including research design, original data collection, and thesis writing.

---

### Comparing the Three Approaches

The fundamental divergence is one of purpose. The EU regulates AI in education primarily as a matter of fundamental rights — student autonomy, non-discrimination, and freedom from surveillance — and uses legal prohibition and conformity assessment to enforce those values. The United States treats the problem primarily as a governance challenge to be managed at the most local level capable of acting, with the federal role confined to incentives and workforce framing. China treats AI integration as a national strategic imperative, with the state setting both the pace and the curriculum, while embedding data security and anti-dependency rules as safety val

What guidance have major international bodies (UNESCO, OECD, EU, and others) issued on AI in education, and how is it being implemented?

International guidance on artificial intelligence in education has matured rapidly since 2019, evolving from broad declarations of principle into operational frameworks, competency standards, and binding regulation. Three institutional actors dominate the landscape — UNESCO, the OECD, and the European Union — each approaching the challenge from a distinct vantage point: normative standard-setting, evidence-based policy analysis, and regulatory governance respectively.

UNESCO: from the Beijing Consensus to competency frameworks. UNESCO's Beijing Consensus on Artificial Intelligence and Education (2019) was the first global instrument on the topic. Adopted at the International Conference on AI and Education, it committed more than one hundred Member States to a human-centred vision in which AI is controlled by humans, protects human rights, and serves the Education 2030 Agenda (Sustainable Development Goal 4). The Consensus insisted that AI should empower rather than replace teachers, that applications must be inclusive and non-discriminatory, and that educational data use should be ethical, transparent, and auditable, with privacy-by-design principles. It also tasked UNESCO with building an AI for Education platform and monitoring the AI divide between nations.

Two years later, UNESCO published AI and Education: Guidance for Policy-makers (2021), the first comprehensive policy manual for governments. The guidance distinguishes three policy approaches — independent (a standalone AI policy), integrated (AI embedded in broader digital transformation strategies), and thematic (AI addressed through specific educational challenges) — and urges policy-makers to become AI-ready before drafting rules. Its priorities include equitable access, ethics and privacy, teacher empowerment, collaborative ecosystems, and investment in infrastructure and research.

In 2024 UNESCO released the AI Competency Framework for Teachers and the AI Competency Framework for Students. The teacher framework organizes five dimensions — human-centred mindset, ethics of AI, AI foundations and applications, AI pedagogy, and AI for professional learning — across three progression levels (Acquire, Deepen, Create). The student framework defines twelve competencies across four dimensions (human-centred mindset, ethics, techniques and applications, and system design) at three levels (Understand, Apply, Create). Both frameworks are explicitly designed to be adapted by national authorities and teacher-education institutions, and they align with existing digital competence standards such as Europe's DigCompEdu.

OECD: evidence, governance, and guardrails. The OECD's Digital Education Outlook 2023 provides the most systematic comparative analysis of AI in education to date. Structured around digital infrastructure, governance, and opportunities for AI-enabled learning, the report found that as of early 2024 none of the eighteen jurisdictions surveyed had issued specific regulations for generative AI in education. The OECD therefore advocates governance, guidelines, and guardrails rather than waiting for legislation, and documents that roughly one-third of teachers already use AI for work-related tasks, chiefly lesson planning and research. The OECD's Education Policy Outlook and its AI policy observatory continue to track how national systems balance teacher supply, digital equity, and AI adoption.

The European Union: binding regulation and strategic planning. The EU has gone furthest in converting guidance into law. The AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) classifies AI systems used in education and vocational training as high-risk under Annex III when they determine access to education, assess learning outcomes, or monitor student behaviour during assessments. High-risk obligations — risk management, data governance, transparency, human oversight, and logging — apply from December 2027, while the ban on emotion recognition in educational settings and the duty to support AI literacy among staff applied from February 2025. The Digital Education Action Plan (2021–2027) complements the Act with ethical guidelines on the use of AI and data in teaching and learning, updates to the DigComp competence frameworks, and the European Digital Education Hub, which coordinates member-state practice and hosts working groups such as the squad on an ethical AI toolkit for schools.

Other international actors and the direction of travel. Beyond the three principal bodies, the Council of Europe has issued guidance on AI and education grounded in human rights and the rule of law, the G20 has endorsed principles for AI in education in its education ministers' communiqués, and regional bodies such as the African Union have begun to develop continental frameworks. The proliferation of instruments has itself become a coordination problem: governments now navigate overlapping guidance from UNESCO, the OECD, the EU, and regional organizations, each with different emphases and legal force. The emerging response is convergence on a common core — human-centred design, teacher agency, equity, privacy, and transparency — with national implementation varying according to legal tradition and institutional capacity. The most significant development is the shift from guidance to obligation: the EU AI Act demonstrates that the era of purely voluntary frameworks is ending, and other jurisdictions, including Brazil and South Korea, are moving toward binding AI legislation with education-specific provisions. The practical implication for education systems is that international guidance now functions less as a menu of options and more as a floor of expectations against which national policy will be judged.

Implementation in practice. Implementation remains uneven across and within countries. Some governments embed AI in national education strategies and teacher-training programmes built on the UNESCO frameworks; EU member states are aligning procurement and institutional practice with the AI Act's timeline; and the OECD continues to document wide variation in teacher readiness and infrastructure. The common thread across all three bodies is a human-centred, rights-based orientation: AI is to augment human judgement, not replace it, and equity, privacy, and teacher agency are treated as non-negotiable conditions of adoption. The principal implementation gaps are teacher preparation, evaluation of learning outcomes, and the digital divide between and within countries — precisely the areas the next generation of guidance, including the UNESCO competency frameworks and the OECD's 2026 Digital Education Outlook, is now seeking to close.

  • AI Competency Framework for Students (UNESCO, 2024) (government) [high credibility]

What does an effective institutional AI policy contain, and what distinguishes policies that work from those ignored in practice?

Institutional AI policy in education has shifted from reactive, ad-hoc measures — typically emergency bans issued when generative AI first became widely available — toward comprehensive, cross-functional governance frameworks. The evidence from institutions that have moved through this transition suggests that effective policies share a recognizable anatomy, and that the difference between a policy that shapes practice and one that is quietly ignored is largely a matter of process, specificity, and enforcement design.

The core components of an effective policy. A well-constructed institutional AI policy typically contains six interlocking elements. First, a governance structure: a standing committee or task force that includes academic leadership, faculty, students, IT, legal, and privacy officers, with a clear mandate to review, update, and interpret the policy over time. Second, a tiered acceptable-use framework that differentiates between low-risk activities (brainstorming, summarising, drafting assistance) and high-stakes decisions (grading, admissions, clinical or legal judgements), rather than a single undifferentiated rule. Third, data governance provisions that define what student and staff data may be entered into which systems, prohibit feeding sensitive institutional data into public, unvetted models, and specify retention and deletion obligations in line with FERPA, GDPR, and emerging AI regulation. Fourth, procurement and vendor due-diligence requirements, including data-processing agreements, termination rights, and bias-mitigation commitments. Fifth, a professional-development and student-literacy component, because a policy that assumes competence it does not build will fail. Sixth, a human-in-the-loop principle stating that AI-generated outputs affecting assessment or administrative decisions must be reviewed and approved by qualified humans.

What distinguishes policies that work. Comparative analyses of institutional practice point to several recurring features of policies that are actually followed. The first is mission alignment: policies framed around the institution's own values and educational purposes — rather than copied from a peer institution or drafted to satisfy external pressure — generate genuine ownership. The second is shared governance: involving faculty and student representatives in drafting from the outset, which builds legitimacy and durable compliance. The third is clarity and specificity: working policies tell a teacher or student exactly what is permitted in a given context, with concrete examples, rather than offering abstract principles that require legal interpretation. The fourth is feasibility of enforcement: policies that rely on detection tools as the sole evidence of misconduct fail, because AI detectors are demonstrably unreliable and produce false positives, particularly for non-native speakers; policies that anchor enforcement in process evidence — drafts, oral review, version histories — are both fairer and more workable. The fifth is a review cycle: because the technology and the regulatory environment change quickly, effective institutions commit to annual policy review rather than treating the document as settled.

Why policies fail in practice. The literature on policy implementation identifies a consistent set of failure modes. Top-down drafting without faculty or student input produces documents that are technically correct but normatively empty — nobody feels bound by them. Vague or maximalist prohibitions (a blanket ban on all AI use) are unenforceable and are widely circumvented, which corrodes the credibility of the entire policy. Punitive framing that treats students as suspects generates resistance and drives AI use underground rather than making it transparent. Policies that mandate compliance without providing training, tools, or support place the burden on individuals who lack the capacity to comply. And policies that are never revisited quickly become obsolete, describing a technology landscape that no longer exists.

What a working policy looks like in practice. Concrete examples help distinguish the abstract from the operational. A tiered acceptable-use framework, for instance, typically defines three bands: unrestricted use (brainstorming, summarising, drafting assistance, with disclosure), restricted use (AI permitted for specific tasks defined in the assignment, such as generating practice problems or providing feedback on drafts), and prohibited use (AI in high-stakes assessment, in final submissions where the assignment requires independent work, or in any context where the student's own competence is what is being certified). Each band is accompanied by examples and by the disclosure requirement that applies to it. Data governance provisions, in turn, specify not only what may be entered into which systems but also who is responsible when a teacher or student violates the rule — a question that institutions frequently leave unresolved. And the human-in-the-loop principle is operationalized through sign-off requirements: AI-generated feedback, reports, or recommendations affecting students must be reviewed and approved by a named human before they take effect. Institutions that publish such concrete specifications report that compliance is higher and disputes are fewer, because the policy answers the questions that actually arise in classrooms rather than the questions that arise in committee.

The emerging consensus. The direction of travel in institutional practice is toward what one strand of the literature calls AI-ready governance: tiered risk classification, explicit human oversight, transparent disclosure norms, and continuous monitoring, embedded in a governance structure that can adapt. The UNESCO AI Competency Framework for Teachers reinforces this by treating institutional support — not individual teacher initiative — as the precondition for ethical AI use. The practical lesson for institutions is that a policy is a living governance instrument, not a document: its effectiveness is determined less by its wording than by the committee that owns it, the training that accompanies it, and the review cycle that keeps it current.

How are education systems regulating student data privacy in AI tools, and which legal frameworks apply to minors?

Student data privacy in the age of AI is regulated through a layered patchwork of general data-protection law, child-specific statutes, and education-specific rules. Because AI tools are data-hungry by design — they ingest prompts, essays, interaction logs, and behavioural signals — the frameworks written for an earlier generation of educational software are being stretched, and regulators and schools are responding with new interpretive guidance and contractual practice.

The United States: FERPA and COPPA. In the United States, two federal statutes carry most of the weight. The Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA) protects education records — any information directly related to a student that is maintained by a school or its agent — and applies to all institutions receiving federal funding. When an AI tool processes, stores, or generates content based on education records, FERPA is triggered. Schools may share records with vendors without parental consent under the school-official exception, but only if the vendor performs an institutional service, remains under the school's direct control, and uses the data solely for the authorized educational purpose. In practice this requires a data-processing agreement (DPA) that mandates purpose limitation, prohibits the use of student data for model training or commercial purposes, and includes deletion, breach-notification, and audit provisions. The Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA) regulates the online collection of personal information from children under thirteen by third-party operators. Vendors must generally obtain verifiable parental consent, but schools may consent on behalf of parents for educational — not commercial — purposes. If a tool uses student data for advertising, behavioural profiling, or model training, the school cannot consent on the parent's behalf; separate parental consent is required. Roughly forty US states have added their own student-privacy statutes, many of them stricter than federal law, with requirements such as public data inventories, prohibitions on selling student data, and specific breach-notification timelines.

The European Union: GDPR and the AI Act. In the EU, the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) applies to any school or provider processing the data of EU residents. Its core principles — lawfulness, purpose limitation, data minimization, and storage limitation — constrain what AI tools may collect and retain, and its provisions on automated decision-making and profiling give students and parents rights to human intervention and to contest decisions. Data Protection Impact Assessments are required for high-risk processing, which AI-based assessment and monitoring tools typically constitute. The GDPR treats children as a specially protected category, and member states may set a lower age of consent for information-society services (commonly sixteen, with a thirteen-to-sixteen range). The EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689) layers on top: AI systems that determine access to education, assess learning outcomes, or monitor student behaviour are high-risk, and from December 2027 their deployers must meet transparency, human-oversight, and logging obligations, while emotion recognition in educational settings is prohibited outright. The UK GDPR and the UK's own AI governance approach mirror much of this structure post-Brexit.

Other jurisdictions. Beyond the US and EU, a growing number of systems regulate student data in AI contexts. Brazil's LGPD, South Korea's AI Basic Act, and China's Personal Information Protection Law and regulations on minors' online protection all impose consent, minimization, and special-protection duties that bear directly on educational AI. The common pattern is that minors attract heightened protection: stricter consent requirements, bans on profiling and behavioural advertising, and limits on automated decisions affecting their education.

Enforcement and the direction of regulatory practice. The frameworks are only as strong as their enforcement, and enforcement is intensifying. Regulators have begun to act against the practices the frameworks prohibit: the US Federal Trade Commission has pursued companies for deceptive data practices involving children, and European data-protection authorities have issued significant fines under the GDPR for unlawful processing of children's data. The practical consequence for schools is that compliance can no longer be treated as a form-filling exercise. Data-processing agreements are being scrutinized as substantive documents: regulators and courts look at whether the school actually exercises the direct control that the school-official exception requires, whether the vendor's sub-processors are disclosed, and whether deletion obligations are technically feasible. The emerging standard is that a school cannot outsource its privacy obligations to a vendor's terms of service; it must negotiate, document, and audit. For minors specifically, the direction of travel is toward heightened protection: stricter consent requirements, prohibitions on profiling and behavioural advertising, and limits on automated decisions affecting education, with the EU AI Act's ban on emotion recognition in educational settings as the most visible example.

AI-specific risks and regulatory responses. The distinctive risks of AI have forced regulators and schools beyond the original design of these statutes. Model training is the first: student inputs may be absorbed into model weights, which is generally a violation of purpose limitation under FERPA and GDPR, and is technically difficult to reverse when a deletion request arrives. Inference and profiling are the second: AI tools can infer sensitive attributes — learning disabilities, emotional states — that the student never disclosed, and these inferences may themselves become protected education records. Unauthorized adoption is the third: teachers using consumer-grade tools without district vetting create unauthorized disclosures of education records. The regulatory response has been procedural: rigorous vendor vetting with signed DPAs, data-flow audits, role-based privacy training, and human-in-the-loop review of AI-generated reports affecting grading, placement, or discipline. The direction of travel is clear — student data protection is moving from a compliance afterthought to a precondition for any AI deployment in education, with the burden of proof placed on vendors and institutions rather than on students and parents.

How should institutions handle procurement of AI tools — evaluation criteria, vendor accountability, contract terms, and exit strategies?

Procurement has become the most consequential governance lever available to education institutions in the AI era. Because AI tools are not neutral software — they make probabilistic judgements, learn from the data they process, and can change behaviour through model updates — standard software acquisition practice is insufficient. The emerging consensus across government procurement handbooks, legal guidance, and institutional practice is that AI procurement must be treated as a governance process with four distinct phases: evaluation, accountability, contracting, and exit.

Evaluation criteria. Before any tool is acquired, institutions should assess it against criteria that go beyond functionality and price. Pedagogical alignment comes first: does the tool serve a defined learning objective, and is there evidence of effectiveness rather than vendor claims? Security and privacy follow: the Higher Education Community Vendor Assessment Toolkit (HECVAT) has become the de facto standard for evaluating vendor security, privacy, and accessibility posture, and institutions increasingly refuse to consider vendors that will not complete it. Data governance is a separate criterion: where data is stored, whether it is used for model training, which sub-processors are involved, and whether deletion is technically feasible. Equity and bias are now standard evaluation dimensions: vendors must be able to document bias-testing and mitigation, and institutions increasingly require accessibility compliance and evidence that the tool does not disadvantage particular student groups. Transparency is the final criterion: institutions need to know what the model does, how it was trained, and how it will be updated, because a black-box tool cannot be governed.

Vendor accountability. Accountability does not end at signature. Institutions are adopting vendor relationship management practices that include scheduled performance reviews, monitoring of AI-specific key performance indicators (accuracy, fairness, uptime), and bias and accessibility audits in real-world settings rather than in vendor test environments. Contracts increasingly require vendors to provide interfaces that allow the client to exercise effective oversight — logging, audit trails, and human-review workflows. The concept of performance drift is central: a model that performed acceptably at deployment may degrade or change after retraining, so accountability mechanisms must be continuous rather than point-in-time.

Contract terms. Legal guidance on AI contracts emphasizes several provisions that differ from conventional software terms. Liability and indemnification are the first battleground: AI vendors routinely cap liability and shift risk to the customer, and institutions are advised to negotiate risk-adjusted liability models that reflect the AI's role in high-stakes decisions affecting students. Data rights are the second: nearly all AI vendors claim rights to use customer data for model retraining or competitive intelligence, and contracts must define data ownership, retention, and deletion with precision. Performance warranties are the third: because AI is probabilistic, traditional software warranties are inadequate; contracts should specify measurable performance standards, reliability targets, and remedies such as service credits or mandatory model retraining when standards are missed. Transparency and compliance commitments — disclosure of training data provenance, model update notification, and explicit compliance with GDPR, FERPA, COPPA, and the EU AI Act — round out the core terms.

Exit strategies. Vendor lock-in is a recognized failure mode of educational technology, and AI intensifies it because models and data pipelines are difficult to port. Effective procurement therefore includes exit strategies from the outset: data portability and export rights in machine-readable formats, transition plans that specify how the institution will migrate to an alternative provider, termination-for-cause and termination-for-convenience clauses, and contractual obligations to delete institutional data on exit. Some jurisdictions now publish AI procurement handbooks that make these elements mandatory for public institutions, and school districts are beginning to publish their own vendor guidance requiring compliance with these standards as a condition of doing business.

Evaluation in practice: pilots and sandboxes. The evaluation phase is increasingly conducted through structured pilots rather than paper assessments. Institutions are adopting sandbox approaches in which a tool is deployed to a limited cohort under defined conditions — with data-collection protocols, success metrics, and a pre-agreed decision point — before any institution-wide commitment. This reflects the reality that paper evaluation cannot predict real-world performance: a tool that scores well on vendor benchmarks may fail in a specific classroom context, with specific students, under specific network conditions. Pilot designs typically specify the evidence that will justify scale-up: learning-outcome data, teacher and student experience, technical reliability, and equity effects across student subgroups. The EU AI Act reinforces this discipline by requiring high-risk education systems to meet risk-management, data-governance, and human-oversight obligations before deployment, and by requiring deployers to conduct fundamental-rights impact assessments. The practical lesson is that procurement decisions should be reversible: a pilot that fails should be as easy to terminate as a pilot that succeeds, which is why the exit-strategy provisions described above must be in place before the pilot begins, not after the institution has become dependent on the tool.

The governance frame. The most important shift is procedural: AI purchases are routed through a centralized governance gate rather than left to individual departments or teachers. This ensures that every acquisition is assessed against institutional principles — safety, equity, transparency, and data protection — before it reaches a classroom. The practical lesson is that procurement is not a transaction but a lifecycle: evaluation, accountability, contracting, and exit must be designed together, because a contract negotiated without an exit strategy, or an evaluation that ignores bias, will fail at the point of greatest cost.

How are examination and credentialing bodies redesigning high-stakes assessment for the AI era?

High-stakes assessment — the examinations and credentials that determine university admission, professional licensure, and certification — has been the most exposed flank of education in the generative AI era. Because these assessments are designed to certify individual competence, an AI system that can produce indistinguishable work threatens their validity at the root. Examination and credentialing bodies have responded with a combination of policy revision, assessment redesign, and a deliberate retreat from detection-based enforcement.

The International Baccalaureate as a case study. The International Baccalaureate (IB) has been among the most explicit of the major credentialing bodies. Rather than banning AI, the IB revised its Academic Integrity policy to include dedicated guidance on AI tools (Appendix 6), built on the principle of transparency. Students who use AI to generate text, images, or other products must cite the tool in the body of their work and include it in their bibliography. The IB places the burden of authentication on teachers, who are expected to use professional judgement to confirm that submitted coursework is the student's own — and it explicitly states that teachers do not need to prove AI use, only to be confident the work reflects the student's capabilities. Where authenticity is in doubt, the IB endorses oral examinations and vivas, process-based evidence such as drafts and version histories, and redoing tasks under controlled conditions. The organization has also published guidance on thirteen common scenarios of AI use in coursework, distinguishing acceptable uses (research, summarising counter-positions, generating structure templates, with acknowledgement) from misconduct (asking AI to write essays, translate entire assessments, or fabricate quotations).

The shift from detection to design. The broader research literature on assessment in the AI era has converged on a diagnosis: detection-based countermeasures are unreliable and inequitable. AI detectors produce variable accuracy, significant false-positive rates, and systematic bias against non-native English speakers, and they fuel an arms race in which students refine evasion techniques. Credentialing bodies are therefore redesigning assessment so that genuine learning is the most effective path to success. A leading conceptual framework organizes this redesign into four pillars: process-based documentation (annotated bibliographies, iterative drafts, research journals that make the learning trajectory visible); oral defence integration (vivas, presentations with questioning, follow-up discussions that require real-time justification of reasoning); authentic task design (tasks anchored in local datasets, seminar discussions, or personal experience that generic AI output cannot satisfy); and transparent AI-use policies (disclosure of prompts, reflection on AI output, and defined boundaries of legitimate use).

Operationalizing redesign at scale. For credentialing bodies, the challenge is scale: oral components and process documentation are labour-intensive compared with machine-marked multiple-choice papers. Institutions are responding with structured oral protocols and standardized rubrics that weight oral defence and process engagement equally with the final product, and with learning-design tools that let students practise oral assessment before high-stakes encounters. Some bodies are integrating oral components as follow-ups to written submissions, asking candidates to walk through their arguments to verify understanding. The IB's approach — teacher authentication supported by oral verification and process evidence — is a template for how a global credentialing body can operationalize these principles without abandoning the scale of its examinations.

Beyond the IB: the wider credentialing landscape. The IB's approach is the most fully articulated, but it is not alone. Other international examination boards and national credentialing bodies have moved in the same direction, revising academic-integrity policies to require disclosure of AI use, strengthening authentication procedures for coursework, and expanding the role of supervised and oral components in high-stakes assessment. The common constraint is economic: oral assessment and process verification are labour-intensive, and bodies that certify hundreds of thousands of candidates annually cannot simply convert their examinations to vivas. The emerging compromise is a portfolio model in which a limited number of high-stakes components — the ones that certify individual competence — are redesigned for AI-resilience, while the majority of assessment continues in supervised, controlled conditions. Digital assessment infrastructure is the enabling condition: secure platforms that support oral components, process documentation, and version histories at scale. The longer-term question is whether the credential itself will change — whether certification will come to rest on a documented trajectory of learning and verified performance rather than on a single examination event. The IB's teacher-authentication model, the research literature's four-pillar framework, and the economics of scale all point toward that direction, but the transition is likely to be gradual and uneven across jurisdictions.

The limits of the redesign. It would be misleading to suggest that the redesign is complete. Many credentialing bodies continue to rely on supervised, closed-book examinations for core components, and the economics of oral assessment at national scale remain unresolved. The direction of travel, however, is consistent across the IB, the research literature, and institutional practice: high-stakes assessment is being rebuilt around the verification of process and the demonstration of reasoning under human questioning, with AI treated as a tool whose use must be transparent rather than a threat to be detected. The long-term question — whether credentials themselves will shift from certifying a single examination performance to certifying a documented trajectory of learning — is the frontier the next generation of assessment design will have to answer.

  • Assessment and Evaluation in Higher Education: AI-Resilient Assessment (Taylor & Francis) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Artificial Intelligence in Learning, Teaching, and Assessment — International Baccalaureate (institutional) [high credibility]

What academic-integrity frameworks have institutions adopted to replace binary "cheating/not cheating" models, and how are they working?

The binary model of academic integrity — an act is either cheating or not, and detection followed by punishment is the enforcement mechanism — has proven structurally inadequate for the generative AI era. AI-assisted work does not map cleanly onto the categories of plagiarism and contract cheating that the binary model was built to police, and the detection tools that sustained it are demonstrably unreliable. Institutions have responded by adopting frameworks that replace the binary with graduated, contextual, and educational models of integrity.

Why the binary model failed. The research literature identifies three compounding failures. First, AI detectors are inaccurate: they produce false positives, particularly for non-native English speakers, and their accuracy varies unpredictably across texts, making them unsafe as the sole basis for disciplinary action. Second, detection fuels an arms race: as detection improves, evasion techniques — paraphrasing, prompt engineering, hybrid human-AI drafting — evolve alongside it, consuming institutional resources and corroding trust between students and faculty. Third, the binary model misclassifies the phenomenon: much AI use in student work is neither straightforward cheating nor legitimate independent work, but a spectrum of assistance that the binary cannot represent. Scholars of contract cheating, notably Tracy Bretag and colleagues, had already argued before the AI era that punitive, surveillance-based approaches fail and that effective integrity policy must be fair, clear, consistent, and educational.

The frameworks that replaced it. Three families of frameworks have emerged. The first is the disclosure-based model, now the dominant approach in higher education: AI use is permitted under defined conditions, and the violation is undisclosed use rather than use itself. Students disclose the tool, the task for which it was used, and the extent of its contribution, and institutions treat failure to disclose as the primary academic offence. The second is the tiered or contextual model, in which the legitimacy of AI use is defined at the level of the course and the assignment rather than by a single institutional rule: some tasks permit AI assistance, others prohibit it, and the syllabus specifies which regime applies. The third is the process-based model, which shifts the evidentiary basis of integrity from the final product to the trajectory of work: iterative drafts, version histories, research journals, and oral defence provide evidence of genuine engagement that a final text alone cannot supply.

Theoretical grounding. These frameworks are increasingly grounded in extended versions of the Theory of Planned Behaviour, which explains academic misconduct as a function of attitudes, subjective norms, and perceived behavioural control. In the AI context, researchers have added technology-specific constructs: perceived instructor involvement (how faculty engagement shapes student attitudes), detection awareness (which can deter or, perversely, drive more sophisticated evasion), and perceived risk of AI use. The implication is that integrity is shaped by the learning environment — by norms, relationships, and the perceived legitimacy of assessment — rather than by the threat of detection alone. This is why the most successful frameworks are pedagogical: they treat AI literacy, disclosure practice, and assessment validity as components of the curriculum rather than as compliance obligations.

Institutional experience and the evidence so far. The survey evidence on how these frameworks are working is instructive. Studies of students in the AI era report near-universal use of AI tools — in some national contexts above ninety per cent of undergraduates — which has made prohibition-based policies untenable and has accelerated the shift to disclosure-based models. Institutions that adopted disclosure frameworks report that students disclose more readily when the policy is specific about what must be disclosed and when instructors model disclosure themselves, and that the quality of integrity conversations improves when the framework is framed educationally rather than punitively. The persistent problems are also documented: students remain uncertain about what counts as disclosable use, particularly at the boundary between assistance and authorship; faculty apply course-level policies inconsistently; and the absence of reliable detection tools means that enforcement continues to rest on process evidence and oral review, which are resource-intensive. The research consensus is that the frameworks work when they are embedded in a wider system of assessment redesign and faculty development, and that they fail when adopted as standalone documents. The measure of success is not the number of cases detected but the proportion of students who can articulate, when asked, what they used AI for and why it was legitimate — the outcome that process-based integrity is designed to produce.

How they are working. The evidence is mixed but instructive. Surveys indicate very high rates of student AI use — in some national contexts above ninety per cent of undergraduates — which makes prohibition-based policies unenforceable and lends credibility to disclosure-based alternatives. Institutions report that disclosure frameworks generate more honest conversations about AI use and reduce the adversarial dynamic of detection, but they also report implementation challenges: disclosure rates remain uneven, students are often unsure what counts as disclosable use, and faculty vary widely in how they apply course-level policies. The research consensus is that the frameworks work when they are accompanied by assessment redesign and faculty development, and fail when they are adopted as policy documents without changing the assessment practices that create the temptation to cheat. The direction of travel is toward what the literature calls rehumanized, validity-driven assessment: integrity frameworks that make honest engagement the most rational strategy for students, supported by process evidence and oral verification rather than by surveillance.

  • AI-Assisted Academic Misconduct and Integrity Frameworks (Education and Information Technologies, Springer) (academic) [high credibility]
  • From Detection to Design: Rehumanizing Academic Integrity in the AI Era (Journal of Academic Ethics, Springer) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Contract Cheating: A New Challenge for Academic Honesty (Bretag, 2013) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Academic Integrity and AI: Sociotechnical Perspectives (Social Sciences, MDPI) (academic) [high credibility]
  • International Center for Academic Integrity (ICAI) — Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (institutional) [high credibility]

How are teacher unions, professional bodies, and accreditation agencies responding to AI, and what standards are they setting?

Teacher unions, professional bodies, and accreditation agencies occupy different positions in the education system, and their responses to AI reflect those positions: unions bargain for the conditions and rights of educators, professional bodies define competence and practice standards, and accreditation agencies enforce quality thresholds. Together they are constructing the normative scaffolding — what teachers should know, what institutions must guarantee, and what rights cannot be traded away — within which AI adoption will proceed.

Teacher unions: rights, guardrails, and partnerships. The major teacher unions have moved from initial alarm to a posture of conditional engagement. The American Federation of Teachers (AFT) launched the National Academy for AI Instruction, a multi-million-dollar initiative developed with Microsoft, OpenAI, and Anthropic, to provide training and guardrails for educators, and has promoted what it calls Commonsense Guardrails for Using Advanced Technology in Schools, which call for continuous assessment of AI tools to prevent bias and protect equity. The National Education Association (NEA) has established an AI in Education Hub to build AI literacy and leadership among its members. Education International, the global federation of teacher unions, has framed AI within its broader defence of the teaching profession, insisting that technology must not be used to erode teachers' working conditions or replace professional judgement. The union response is not without internal tension: critics within and outside the labour movement have questioned the scale of partnerships with technology companies, the adequacy of rank-and-file involvement in decisions about those partnerships, and whether equity frameworks embedded in AI guidance serve educational or ideological purposes. What is not in dispute is the core union demand: AI must augment teachers, not displace them, and its introduction must be negotiated rather than imposed.

Professional bodies: competency standards. The most consequential standard-setting has come from professional and intergovernmental bodies. UNESCO's AI Competency Framework for Teachers (2024) is the reference point: five dimensions — human-centred mindset, ethics of AI, AI foundations and applications, AI pedagogy, and AI for professional learning — across three progression levels (Acquire, Deepen, Create). The framework is explicit that teachers must not lose core competencies through over-reliance on AI)Skip, and that human accountability must be preserved. It is designed to be adapted by national authorities into training programmes and certification requirements. The OECD's work on reimagining teaching in an accelerating world similarly treats AI competence as a component of the teaching profession's future, while Europe's DigCompEdu framework provides a digital-competence standard for educators that has been updated to incorporate AI and data literacy. These frameworks are converging on a common set of standards: teachers must understand how AI works, evaluate its outputs critically, use it ethically, protect student data, and retain final responsibility for pedagogical decisions.

Accreditation agencies: quality thresholds. Accreditation agencies have been slower and more varied in their response, but a pattern is emerging. Regional accreditors in the United States and quality-assurance bodies in Europe are beginning to require institutions to demonstrate that they have AI policies, faculty development, and assessment practices that preserve academic integrity and learning outcomes. The emphasis is on institutional capacity rather than on prescribing particular tools: an institution seeking accreditation must show that it governs AI use, protects students, and can evidence that its qualifications still certify genuine learning. The EU AI Act adds a regulatory floor, classifying AI used in admissions, assessment, and monitoring as high-risk and requiring compliance from December 2027, which accreditation and quality-assurance bodies are beginning to incorporate into their standards.

Accreditation and quality assurance in practice. The response of accreditation agencies is becoming visible in concrete requirements. Regional accreditors in the United States are beginning to ask institutions to document their AI governance — policies, faculty development, and assessment practices — as part of accreditation reviews, and European quality-assurance bodies are incorporating AI competence and integrity provisions into their standards. The emphasis is on institutional capacity and evidence: an institution must be able to show that it governs AI use, protects student data, and can demonstrate that its qualifications still certify genuine learning. The EU AI Act provides the regulatory floor, and quality-assurance frameworks are beginning to reference it explicitly. Professional bodies beyond UNESCO are also active: technology-education associations have issued AI standards for teachers and students, and subject-specific professional bodies are revising their codes of practice to address AI use in their disciplines. The convergence is toward a professional standard with three elements: competence (teachers can use and evaluate AI), ethics (teachers protect students, data, and equity), and accountability (teachers retain responsibility for decisions, with AI as an instrument rather than an authority). The unresolved tension remains the distributional one — whether the partnerships between unions and technology companies, and the standards set by bodies with different constituencies, will serve the profession's interests or the vendors' — and it will be resolved in practice by the vigilance of the memberships these bodies represent.

The emerging standard. Across all three groups, a consistent standard is taking shape: human oversight of AI decisions, protection of teacher professional judgement and working conditions, equity and data protection as non-negotiable conditions, and demonstrable institutional capacity — policy, training, and assessment design — as the price of legitimacy. The unresolved questions are distributional: whether the partnerships unions have formed with technology companies will shift power within the profession, and whether accreditation standards will be enforced with the rigour that their language implies.

  • Reimagining Teaching in an Accelerating World (OECD) (government) [high credibility]
  • DigCompEdu: Digital Competence Framework for Educators (European Commission JRC) (government) [high credibility]
  • Education International: Global Education Union Movement and the Defence of Educators (institutional) [high credibility]

What funding models and public investments in AI for education exist, and what do early returns suggest about their effectiveness?

Public investment in AI for education has moved from pilot projects to structured funding models, and the early evidence on returns is beginning to separate what works from what merely spends. The funding landscape spans national AI strategies, dedicated research and infrastructure programmes, and new contracting models designed to align vendor incentives with learning outcomes.

The funding models. Four models dominate. The first is the national-strategy model, in which governments embed education within broader AI investment frameworks. In the United States, the White House AI Action Plan directs agencies including the Departments of Education and Labor to prioritize AI skill development, while the National Science Foundation's TechAccess: AI-Ready America programme (NSF 26-508) commits roughly $168–224 million to coordinate AI readiness through state and territory coordination hubs, a national coordination lead, and catalyst award competitions. The CHIPS and Science Act's $53 billion for semiconductor and research infrastructure underpins the hardware base for AI in education and beyond. The second is the targeted-intervention model, in which governments fund specific AI applications — tutoring, teacher support, adaptive platforms — often through outcome-based or pay-as-you-go contracting rather than traditional procurement, a shift the World Bank explicitly recommends to manage the risk of rapidly evolving technology. The third is the public-private partnership model, in which technology companies co-fund or supply AI tools and training for public education systems, as seen in union and district partnerships with major AI vendors. The fourth is the evidence-generation model, in which investment is deliberately coupled to evaluation: the World Bank argues that evidence generation should be treated as a core investment rather than an afterthought, particularly in low- and middle-income countries where data on scalability and cost-effectiveness is scarce.

What the early returns show. The emerging evidence base, assembled by the World Bank, the OECD, and independent researchers, points in a consistent direction. AI tutoring can produce substantial learning gains at remarkably low cost: a teacher-guided AI tutoring programme in Ecuador achieved significant improvements in mathematics at roughly $18 per student, and a Stanford analysis estimated that AI-enhanced tutoring could scale expert pedagogical strategies at about $20 per tutor annually. A field experiment in Türkiye found that the design of the AI interface mattered enormously: a tutoring version designed to support learning produced a 127 per cent performance improvement, compared with 48 per cent for a standard interface. Teacher-facing AI shows strong returns on administrative burden: secondary science teachers in England experienced a 31 per cent reduction in time spent on lesson and resource planning, and low-experience tutors supported by AI raised student pass rates by nine percentage points. The OECD's Digital Education Outlook 2026 consolidates this picture, emphasizing that AI is most effective when it augments human instruction rather than replaces it.

The cautionary findings. The same evidence base contains clear warnings. In the Turkish experiment, students performed 17 per cent worse once AI access was removed, suggesting that without explicit pedagogical design, AI can substitute for rather than build durable skills. The OECD and World Bank both warn against AI as a shortcut: overreliance on tools that provide direct answers reduces cognitive effort and active engagement. The digital divide remains the structural constraint — investments that assume universal connectivity and device access risk widening rather than narrowing inequity, particularly in low- and middle-income settings. And the history of educational technology spending, in which large sums produced no measurable improvement in outcomes, counsels against assuming that expenditure equals effectiveness.

International variation and the OECD synthesis. The funding landscape varies substantially across jurisdictions. Some governments have created dedicated national programmes for AI in education, others have folded AI into broader digital-education strategies, and still others have left investment primarily to the private sector — a variation that the OECD's Digital Education Outlook 2026 documents in detail. The OECD synthesis emphasizes that the effectiveness of investment depends less on its size than on its design: investments that augment teachers, that are coupled to evaluation, and that build on existing infrastructure outperform larger investments in hardware and platforms that are not. The World Bank's guidance for low- and middle-income countries is particularly explicit: prioritize teacher-facing interventions, leverage existing devices and mobile networks, fund well-designed pilots before system-wide deployment, and use outcome-based contracting to align vendor incentives with learning outcomes. The early returns also carry a distributional warning: the countries and districts that benefit most from AI investment are those with the strongest infrastructure and teacher capacity, which means that without deliberate design, public investment in AI can widen the very gaps it is intended to close. The emerging consensus is that the binding constraint on effectiveness is not capital but capacity — teacher preparation, evaluation infrastructure, and governance — and that funding models which ignore this constraint will repeat the history of educational technology spending, in which large sums produced little measurable improvement.

The emerging synthesis. The early returns suggest that the most cost-effective investments are teacher-facing (planning, feedback, coaching support) rather than student-facing hardware, that interface and pedagogical design determine whether AI strengthens or weakens learning, and that outcome-based contracting and rigorous evaluation are the mechanisms that keep vendor incentives aligned with student outcomes. The funding models that survive contact with evidence will be those that treat AI as an augmentation of teachers and a subject of continuous evaluation, not as a replacement for either.

  • OECD Digital Education Outlook 2026 (government) [high credibility]

Chapter 11. Chapter 10: Practical Navigation — Guides for Teachers, Students, and Leaders

How should a teacher decide, lesson by lesson, when AI use helps learning and when it undermines it — is there a practical decision framework?

The question of when AI helps and when it undermines learning cannot be answered once, at the level of policy; it must be answered repeatedly, at the level of the individual lesson and task. The research literature offers two kinds of resources: established technology-integration frameworks that predate AI, and emerging AI-specific frameworks that adapt them. Synthesizing these yields a practical decision procedure a teacher can apply in minutes.

The established frameworks. The TPACK framework (Technological, Pedagogical, and Content Knowledge) locates effective technology use at the intersection of what a teacher knows about the subject, about teaching, and about the tool — the implication being that AI use is only sound when the teacher understands how the tool interacts with the specific content and pedagogy of the lesson. The SAMR model (Substitution, Augmentation, Modification, Redefinition) provides a scale: at the lower levels AI replaces or enhances existing practice, while at the higher levels it enables tasks that were previously impossible. The critical insight from SAMR is that substitution — using AI to do what a worksheet or search engine already did — is rarely worth the risks, while redefinition — using AI to create learning experiences that could not otherwise exist — is where the pedagogical value lies.

The AI-specific extensions. Researchers have extended these models for generative AI. Intelligent-TPACK (i-TPACK) adds AI ethics and AI-technological content knowledge to the original domains, requiring teachers to act as ethical gatekeepers who evaluate fairness, transparency, and data privacy. AI-CoACT proposes a four-phase cycle — Awareness, Integration, Collaboration, Transformation — that treats human-AI co-creation as an iterative process of planning, action, and reflection. The PSAMR variant adds a prohibition level to SAMR, acknowledging that some AI uses must be restricted to preserve academic integrity. UNESCO's AI Competency Framework for Teachers organizes the same territory into five dimensions across three progression levels (Acquire, Deepen, Create), with the explicit principle that teachers must not lose core competencies through over-reliance on AI. The OECD's guidance on effective and equitable use of AI in education adds the governance layer: guardrails, human oversight, and equity must be designed into any use.

A practical decision framework. These frameworks can be condensed into a five-question procedure that a teacher can run against any proposed AI use in a lesson. First, the objective question: what is the specific learning objective of this task, and what cognitive work must remain the student's own for that objective to be met? If the objective is to practise argumentation, the argument must be the student's; if the objective is to learn how to interrogate AI output, the AI is the object of study. Second, the enhancement question: does AI use enhance or replace the learning activity? Enhancement — AI as a scaffold, a source of feedback, a generator of practice problems — preserves the student's cognitive work; replacement — AI producing the product the student was meant to produce — eliminates it. Third, the evidence question: is there reason to believe this use improves learning outcomes, or is it novelty? The tutoring literature shows that AI improves learning when it is designed to scaffold, question, and provide feedback, and undermines it when it supplies direct answers. Fourth, the integrity question: is this use transparent, disclosed, and consistent with the course's assessment policy? Undisclosed use, even when pedagogically defensible, corrodes trust. Fifth, the equity question: does every student have equivalent access and support, and does the use disadvantage any group? A tool that advantages students with better access or prompting skills can widen gaps.

A worked example. The framework is best understood through application. Consider a history teacher planning a lesson on the causes of the First World War. The objective question: students must be able to construct and defend a multi-causal argument — the cognitive work that must remain theirs is the synthesis and the argument. The enhancement question: an AI tool that generates a timeline of events, or that quizzes students on the chronology, enhances the lesson by providing practice and scaffolding; an AI tool that produces the essay the students were meant to write replaces the learning. The evidence question: the tutoring literature suggests the quiz and timeline uses are likely to improve outcomes, while the essay-generation use is likely to undermine them. The integrity question: if the essay is a graded assessment, its use must be disclosed and governed by the course policy; the quiz use may require no disclosure at all. The equity question: every student needs equivalent access to the tool and equivalent instruction in using it. The teacher's decision — permit the timeline and quiz, require disclosure for any drafting assistance, prohibit AI-written essays — follows directly from the five questions, and can be explained to students in those terms. That explainability is itself part of the value: students who understand why a use is permitted or prohibited are more likely to comply, and more likely to learn the judgement themselves.

The judgement that remains. The framework does not remove professional judgement; it structures it. The same AI use can be transformative in one lesson and corrosive in another, depending on the objective, the students, and the assessment context. What the frameworks supply is a discipline: teachers who run the five questions — objective, enhancement, evidence, integrity, equity — make decisions that are defensible to students, parents, and colleagues, and that keep the learning objective, rather than the technology, as the unit of analysis. That, in the end, is the practical answer to the lesson-by-lesson question.

What are the current best practices for designing AI-resilient and AI-inclusive assignments and assessments?

The design of assignments and assessments has become the central battleground of academic integrity in the AI era. The research literature and institutional practice have converged on a clear diagnosis: detection-based approaches are unreliable, inequitable, and unsustainable, and the alternative is to design assessments that are AI-resilient — resistant to being completed by AI without genuine learning — and AI-inclusive — explicitly teaching students to use AI well. The two goals are complementary, not contradictory.

The case against detection. AI detectors produce variable accuracy, significant false-positive rates, and systematic bias against non-native English speakers, and they generate an arms race in which students refine evasion techniques. Institutions increasingly advise that detector scores should never serve as the sole evidence of misconduct. The design alternative makes genuine learning the most effective path to success, so that the assessment itself — rather than surveillance — does the work of ensuring integrity.

The four pillars of AI-resilient design. A leading conceptual framework organizes resilient assessment into four pillars. The first is process-based documentation: assessments that make the learning trajectory visible through annotated bibliographies, iterative drafts with reflections at each stage, research journals, and version histories. A final text can be generated by AI; a documented trajectory of drafts, revisions, and reflections is far harder to fabricate. The second is oral defence integration: vivas, presentations with questioning, and follow-up discussions require students to justify and extend their reasoning in real time, providing robust evidence of individual competence that AI cannot supply. The third is authentic task design: assessments anchored in local datasets, recent seminar discussions, personal workplace experience, or other situated contexts that generic AI output cannot satisfy. The fourth is transparent AI-use policies: rather than bans, policies that define legitimate use, require disclosure of prompts, and ask students to reflect on AI output, thereby building digital literacy and ethical responsibility.

AI-inclusive design. The complementary strand of best practice treats AI as a legitimate object and instrument of learning. AI-inclusive assignments teach students to interrogate AI output, verify claims, and use AI as a drafting and feedback tool under defined conditions. Common designs include: assignments that require students to critique an AI-generated text, identifying its errors and biases; tasks in which students must improve on AI output through their own analysis; and process assignments in which students document how they used AI and what they changed. The International Baccalaureate's guidance on thirteen scenarios of AI use in coursework exemplifies this approach at the level of a global credentialing body: acceptable uses (research, summarising counter-positions, structure templates, with acknowledgement) are distinguished from misconduct (AI-written essays, fabricated quotations, translated assessments), and teachers authenticate work through oral questioning and process evidence.

Practical implementation. Institutions that have operationalized these principles report several enabling conditions. Vulnerability audits — systematic reviews of existing assignments to identify which could be completed by AI — are the standard starting point. Rubrics that weight process, oral defence, and reasoning equally with the final product make the design principles enforceable. Structured oral protocols and learning-design tools allow oral components to scale beyond the seminar room. And faculty development is the precondition: teachers need time and training to redesign assessments, and institutions that provide it see the design principles take root, while those that issue guidance without support see them ignored.

Designing for the AI-inclusive classroom. AI-inclusive design is not a retreat from rigour; it is a deliberate expansion of what assessment can measure. Assignments that require students to critique an AI-generated text assess critical evaluation — a skill that is now as fundamental as summarising. Assignments that require students to improve on AI output assess the ability to add value beyond what automation can produce, which is increasingly the definition of expert work. Assignments that require students to document their AI use — what they asked, what they got, what they changed and why — assess metacognition and process awareness. The International Baccalaureate's thirteen-scenario guidance provides a practical map of this territory, distinguishing uses that are acceptable with acknowledgement from those that constitute misconduct. The design principle that unifies these practices is that the assessment should make the student's thinking visible: process documentation makes the trajectory visible, oral defence makes the reasoning visible, authentic tasks make the application visible, and disclosure makes the use of tools visible. Institutions that have adopted this principle report that it changes the character of the student-teacher relationship, replacing the adversarial dynamic of detection with a collaborative dynamic of evidence — a shift that is both pedagogically and ethically significant.

The evidence so far. The early evidence is encouraging but incomplete. Institutions report that process-based and oral assessments generate more authentic evidence of learning and reduce the adversarial dynamic of detection, and the IB's experience suggests that teacher authentication supported by oral verification can work at scale. The open questions are economic — oral assessment at national scale is expensive — and cultural: design-based integrity requires a shift in how institutions and students understand assessment, from a product to be submitted to a process to be evidenced. The direction of travel, however, is unambiguous: the best current practice is to design assessments that make honest, visible engagement the most rational strategy for students, and to treat AI as a tool whose use is taught, disclosed, and evaluated rather than a threat to be detected.

  • Assessment Redesign in the Generative AI Era (PMC / National Library of Medicine) (academic) [high credibility]
