  • AI Dependency in Education: Motivation, Creativity and Learning Outcomes (JMIR Medical Education) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Opportunities, Guidelines and Guardrails for Effective and Equitable Use of AI in Education (OECD) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • Illusion of Competence and Skill Degradation in AI Dependency Among Users (IJRSI) (academic)
  • The Cognitive Cost of Over-Reliance on AI in Education: A Global Review (policy)

What is known about the longitudinal effects of sustained AI use on learners, and how should long-term studies be designed and tracked?

The longitudinal evidence on AI in education is young but already double-edged. Sustained AI use can produce substantial, durable gains in achievement and equity — and, under different conditions, can produce persistent learning deficits. The difference between these outcomes is not the technology itself but how it is designed and governed. Understanding what is known, and designing studies that will tell us more, is one of the most urgent tasks in the field.

What the Evidence Shows: The Gains

The most encouraging longitudinal findings come from large-scale deployments of AI-driven adaptive learning systems. A study of 18,000 students across 12 higher education institutions found that AI-adaptive sections achieved an 88.3% course completion rate compared with 76.5% in traditional sections, with particular gains in skill mastery among students from lower-income and underrepresented backgrounds. In K-12 virtual education, research tracking more than 26,000 students found that initial performance gaps between AI users and non-users narrowed by 91% within a year, suggesting that AI can function as a genuine equalizer when integrated into a well-designed learning environment. These findings matter because they come from sustained use over months, not from short demonstrations, and because they show effects on the outcomes — completion, mastery, equity — that matter most.

What the Evidence Shows: The Risks

The same body of research documents a darker pattern. A three-month study of students using AI tutors found that those with on-demand access achieved less than half the performance gains (30%) of those provided with controlled, system-regulated assistance (64%). The researchers identify a "self-regulation paradox": students recognize that over-relying on AI undermines their long-term development, yet continue to use it as a shortcut during difficult tasks. This over-reliance erodes "productive struggle" — the cognitive process of grappling with challenges that drives expertise — and follow-up testing confirmed that the resulting learning deficits persist beyond the period of use. The persistence finding is the critical one: it means the harm is not a temporary dip but a durable loss. Individual characteristics such as skill level or motivation do not protect against this pattern; even high-achieving students over-rely when given unrestricted access. The design implication is that system-level constraints — rate-limiting, forced delays, adaptive scaffolding that offers help only within a student's zone of proximal development — are more reliable protections than student self-regulation.

What Remains Unknown

For all the progress, the field's unknowns are larger than its knowns. There are no studies that follow learners across the full arc of schooling with sustained AI use, and therefore no direct evidence on the effects of AI-mediated learning on adult outcomes — employment, further education, lifelong learning capacity. The interaction between AI use and developmental stage is unmeasured: the same tool may affect a 9-year-old and a 19-year-old differently, and the longitudinal designs that could detect this do not yet exist. The effects of AI on non-cognitive outcomes — motivation, identity as a learner, social development, attitudes toward effort — are largely unstudied at scale. And the equity question is unresolved in both directions: AI may narrow gaps in some contexts and widen them in others, and the conditions that determine which outcome occurs are not yet understood.

The Governance of Longitudinal Data

Long-term study design is inseparable from data governance. Following learners over years requires retaining educational data — achievement records, usage logs, demographic information — under conditions that protect students while enabling science. This means consent architectures that anticipate long horizons, data retention policies that distinguish research use from operational use, and governance bodies that can oversee a study across institutional changes and staff turnover. It also means confronting the equity dimension of longitudinal data: the students most likely to be lost to follow-up are often those from the most disadvantaged backgrounds, and studies must plan for attrition that is not random. The ethical case for longitudinal research is strong — the field cannot claim to understand sustained AI use without it — but the ethical case depends on governance that students and families can trust, which is itself a design requirement, not an afterthought.

How Long-Term Studies Should Be Designed

Given what is at stake, long-term studies should be designed with several features. First, prospective cohorts: learners followed from a defined starting point, with AI exposure measured continuously rather than dichotomously, so that dose-response relationships can be estimated. Second, active comparison conditions: cohorts using alternative instructional approaches, not merely non-users, so that effects are attributable to AI rather than to any additional support. Third, a broad outcome battery: achievement, transfer, unassisted performance, metacognition, motivation, and equity-relevant outcomes, measured at intervals that include retention checks after AI exposure is removed. Fourth, design variation as a study variable: because the evidence shows that outcomes depend on how AI is deployed, studies should compare deployment conditions — unrestricted versus regulated access, tutor versus tool, scaffolded versus open — rather than treating "AI use" as a single treatment. Fifth, data infrastructure: longitudinal research requires that institutions retain the data needed for follow-up, under governance that protects students while enabling science. The Michigan Virtual longitudinal analysis and the multi-institution adaptive learning studies demonstrate that such infrastructure is feasible. The field will not answer its most important questions through more short-term trials alone; it needs studies long enough, and designed carefully enough, to observe what sustained AI use does to learners over years — and to distinguish the conditions under which it builds them up from those under which it quietly tears them down.

  • Longitudinal Effects of AI-Mediated Learning Environments (Computers and Education: AI) (academic) [high credibility]
  • AI and Student Usage in Online Learning: A Longitudinal Analysis in K-12 Virtual Education (Michigan Virtual) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • Impact of AI-Driven Adaptive Learning Systems on Completion and Mastery (ELQN) (academic)
  • AI-Supported Instruction and Learner Autonomy: Longitudinal Perspectives (Zenodo) (academic)

How should learning analytics generated by AI systems be interpreted, validated, and protected from misuse in student evaluation?

Learning analytics — the use of data mining, machine learning, and AI to monitor and predict student behavior — has moved from research curiosity to institutional infrastructure. The same systems that can identify at-risk students early, personalize support, and improve course design can also misclassify, stigmatize, and unfairly evaluate students when their outputs are misinterpreted or misused. The gap between the technology's promise and its dangers is bridged by three disciplines: interpretation, validation, and protection.

The Interpretive Challenge

Learning analytics outputs are probabilistic inferences, not facts. A risk score is a model's estimate that a student will fail or drop out, derived from historical patterns in data that may not generalize to this student, this course, or this institution. The first interpretive discipline is therefore epistemic humility: analytics describe correlations, not causes, and they describe populations, not individuals. A student flagged as at-risk may be misflagged because the model learned from a cohort unlike them, because the data are incomplete, or because the model is simply wrong. Interpretation must also confront the "lack of full awareness" problem: students are frequently unaware of the extent to which their behavior is being monitored and analyzed, which means they cannot contest or even understand the interpretations placed on their data. Institutions should treat every analytics output as a hypothesis to be investigated, not a verdict to be acted on.

Validation: The Technical Discipline

Validation is the process of establishing that an analytics system measures what it claims to measure and predicts what it claims to predict. This has several components. Predictive validity: risk models should be validated on data not used to build them, and their calibration — whether a predicted 70% risk actually occurs 70% of the time — should be checked and reported. Construct validity: the constructs being measured, such as engagement or effort, should be defined and measured consistently, and the system's proxies for them should be examined for bias. Fairness validation: performance should be evaluated across demographic subgroups, because a model that predicts accurately overall may be systematically wrong for particular groups. And temporal validity: models trained on past cohorts should be re-validated as cohorts, courses, and platforms change, since the patterns analytics learn are not stable. Institutional governance bodies — academic boards, institutional review boards, or dedicated analytics ethics committees — should review and approve analytics systems before deployment)Skip, and codes of ethics in the field emphasize the need to monitor the quality and validity of data processes continuously.

Protection from Misuse in Evaluation

The highest-risk use of learning analytics is in student evaluation, where outputs can affect grades, progression, and access to opportunity. Protection requires several design choices. First, transparency: students should have meaningful and accessible access to the analytics and evaluations derived from their data, including the metrics and labels applied to them, so that they can understand and contest how they are being evaluated. Second, the primacy of direct evidence: analytics should inform, but never replace, direct assessment of student work; a risk score is not evidence of learning, and it should not function as one. Third, effort-based and dynamic factors: evaluation-relevant analytics should prioritize metrics that reflect student effort and that can change over time, rather than static demographic or historical data that unfairly categorize a student's potential. Fourth, human review: consequential decisions should be made by humans who can see the student, the work, and the context that the model cannot. Fifth, prohibition of misuse: analytics data should not be used for purposes beyond the educational function for which it was collected — not for disciplinary surveillance, not for profiling, not for commercial purposes — and this prohibition should be enforceable.

Explainability and Student-Facing Tools

Interpretation and validation are supported by explainability: the capacity to state, in terms a student or teacher can understand, why the system produced a particular output. Explainability is not the same as technical transparency — a student does not need the model's weights, but does need to know what data the system used, what it inferred, and with what confidence. Student-facing analytics dashboards should present this information in accessible form, showing the student what the system believes about their learning and how that belief was formed. The pedagogical value is double: students who can see the system's reasoning can contest it, and students who can contest it are practicing the critical evaluation of algorithmic claims that AI literacy requires. Institutions should treat explainability as a procurement requirement and a design standard, not a vendor courtesy.

The Governance Architecture

Protection ultimately depends on governance. Institutions need a published analytics policy that states what is collected, what is inferred, who can see what, how outputs may and may not be used, and how students can access and contest their data. They need oversight bodies with the authority to audit systems, investigate complaints, and suspend problematic deployments. And they need to treat analytics ethics as a participatory process: students and faculty should be involved in deciding what the institution may infer about learners and what it may do with those inferences. The ethical frameworks developed for learning analytics — which emphasize transparency, student agency, and the monitoring of data quality — provide the normative foundation. The technology will only become more capable, and the interpretations placed on students' data will only become more consequential. Institutions that build the interpretive, validation, and protective disciplines now will be able to use analytics as a tool for student support; those that do not will find themselves using it as an instrument of unaccountable judgment.

  • Institutional Governance of Learning Analytics Ethics (Open Learning) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Learning Analytics Ethics: A Discussion Paper (Melbourne CSHE) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Ethical Considerations in Learning Analytics and Educational Data Mining (ERIC) (academic) [high credibility]

What benchmarks and audits exist for evaluating the pedagogical quality, safety, and bias of educational AI models themselves?

Evaluating an educational AI model is not the same as evaluating an educational intervention. The intervention question asks whether the tool improves learning; the model question asks whether the model itself is pedagogically sound, safe, and fair. The second question has its own emerging toolkit: benchmarks, audits, and fairness metrics, each with known strengths and known limits.

The Limits of Static Benchmarks

Generic AI benchmarks — WEAT, StereoSet, WinoBias, and their successors — measure bias in language models, but they are widely criticized for failing to capture the nuances of decision-level or context-sensitive bias. A model that scores well on a word-association benchmark can still systematically disadvantage particular students when used for admissions, special education referrals, or grading, because those decisions depend on context that static benchmarks do not simulate. The same critique applies to general capability benchmarks: a model's performance on a general knowledge test says little about whether it can tutor a struggling reader or explain a physics concept without introducing misconceptions. Educational evaluation therefore requires benchmarks and audits designed for educational contexts, not borrowed from general AI evaluation.

Audit-Style Evaluation

In response, researchers are developing audit-style frameworks that simulate real-world educational tasks to observe how models behave under controlled conditions. These audits present the model with scenarios — selecting students for awards, identifying at-risk learners, comparing two students with identical performance profiles but different demographic attributes — and measure whether the model exhibits preferences that track demographic cues. This approach isolates the source of bias in a way that static benchmarks cannot, and it produces evidence that is directly relevant to the decisions the model will actually make. The emerging taxonomy of bias in educational AI distinguishes group-level biases (systemic inequalities affecting demographic groups), individual-level biases (unfair treatment of similar students), and multi-level biases (interactions between individual characteristics and demographic patterns) — and audit designs should be capable of detecting all three.

Fairness Metrics and Disparate Impact

Audits need quantitative metrics, and the field has converged on measures of disparate impact rather than aggregate accuracy. Reporting that a model is 92% accurate overall is inadequate when it is 65% accurate for a particular subgroup; fairness evaluation requires metrics such as demographic parity and equalized odds, computed across the subgroups the model will affect. Established libraries — Fairlearn, AIF360 — make these metrics accessible to practitioners, and the regulatory environment is beginning to require them. The U.S. Department of Education's Office for Civil Rights has signaled that AI-driven decisions in federally funded institutions must not produce disparate impacts on protected groups under Title VI, regardless of intent, and that high-stakes systems such as automated placement or special education referral tools should undergo periodic bias audits. The legal standard is shifting from "did the model intend harm" to "did the model produce harm" — which is precisely what disparate-impact metrics measure.

Pedagogical Quality and Safety Benchmarks

Bias is only one dimension of model evaluation. Pedagogical quality requires benchmarks for whether a model's explanations are accurate, age-appropriate, and free of misconceptions; whether its feedback promotes learning rather than merely providing answers; and whether it adapts to learner errors in pedagogically sound ways. Safety requires evaluation of content risks: harmful output, inappropriate content, manipulation, and the model's behavior when students attempt to misuse it. The emerging practice of model cards for education — structured documentation of a model's intended use, capabilities, limitations, and evaluation results — provides a vehicle for making these assessments visible and comparable. The Eedi/LearnLM trial in UK secondary schools offers a model of what rigorous safety evaluation looks like in practice: a post-trial audit of more than 3,600 AI messages found zero instances of harmful content and a 0.1% rate of factual errors, under conditions of human supervision. That is the standard the field should aspire to — not perfection, but measured, reported, and auditable performance.

Third-Party Certification and Regulatory Audits

The audit landscape is becoming institutionalized. Third-party certification bodies are emerging to evaluate educational AI against published standards, and regulatory authorities are beginning to exercise audit powers — the U.S. Department of Education's Office for Civil Rights has signaled that bias audits may be required periodically for high-stakes educational systems, and the EU AI Act's conformity assessment regime will bring a similar discipline to European deployments. The significance for institutions is that audits are becoming a compliance activity, not merely a quality practice: the question is shifting from whether to audit to how audits will be conducted, by whom, and against what standards. Institutions should participate in shaping those standards — through procurement requirements, professional associations, and public comment — because the audit regime that emerges will define what counts as a safe, fair educational AI for the next decade.

The Institutional Role

Benchmarks and audits are necessary but not sufficient; they must be demanded and interpreted by institutions. Because many educational AI systems are proprietary, external audits are essential for accountability, and institutions should require audit results as a condition of procurement. They should also recognize the limits of any single evaluation: models change, contexts differ, and a benchmark result is a snapshot, not a guarantee. The responsible institutional posture is continuous evaluation — benchmarks at procurement, audits at deployment, and re-evaluation as models and contexts evolve — combined with the human oversight that no benchmark can replace.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)
  • A Taxonomy of Bias in Educational AI: Group, Individual and Multi-Level Perspectives (arXiv) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Audit-Style Evaluation of Educational AI: Scenario-Based Bias Detection (Frontiers in Education) (academic) [high credibility]
  • LearnLM: Supervised AI Tutoring in UK Secondary Schools — Safety Audit Findings (arXiv) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Fairness and Effectiveness in AI-Driven Educational Assessments: Challenges and Mitigation Strategies (academic)
  • Bias Concerns in AI for Education: Fairness Metrics and Regulatory Context (industry)

Chapter 13. Chapter 12: Frontiers — Emerging Territories

How are multimodal and voice-based AI changing learning beyond text — in languages, arts, sciences, and physical skills?

The first generation of AI in education was text-centric: chatbots, essay feedback, and text-based tutoring. The second generation is multimodal — systems that process and generate across voice, vision, video, and text — and it is changing what AI can do in domains where text was never the medium of learning. Language, arts, sciences, and physical skills each present different affordances, and each is being transformed in different ways.

Language Learning: The Conversational Turn

Voice-enabled AI has transformed language acquisition, which is fundamentally an oral skill. Learners can now practice target languages through real-time conversation with AI systems that provide immediate feedback on pronunciation, fluency, and comprehension — practice that was previously available only through human conversation partners, which most learners lacked. The technology extends beyond simple dialogue: intelligent avatar-based systems integrate linguistic, paralinguistic, and affective components — gesture generation, facial expressions, and prosodic control — to create engaging, culturally relevant instruction. For learners of less-commonly-taught languages, this is a genuine expansion of opportunity, since the supply of human teachers and conversation partners is thin. The pedagogical question is whether AI conversation partners can develop the pragmatic and cultural competence that human interaction develops — a question that is open, but the practice itself is already widespread.

Sciences and Mathematics: Seeing the Work

Vision-enabled AI has changed what a tutor can see. Text-only systems could only respond to typed input; multimodal systems can interpret handwritten mathematical equations, code diagrams, lab notebook entries, and student drawings, offering feedback on work that was previously invisible to the machine. This matters pedagogically because much of learning in science and mathematics is embodied in representations — equations, graphs, diagrams, experimental records — and the ability to see and respond to those representations brings AI tutoring much closer to what a human tutor does when they look over a student's shoulder. It also enables new assessment possibilities: systems that can observe a student's process, not just their final answer, can diagnose where reasoning goes wrong rather than merely marking the result incorrect.

Arts: Cross-Modal Creation

In the arts, generative AI functions as what one line of research calls a "voicing agent" for transdisciplinary exploration. Cross-modal generation — producing music from paintings, images from musical scores, movement from text — allows students to explore relationships between artistic domains that were previously difficult to bridge. Students can compose, critique, and iterate in media they have not mastered, and can experience the creative process from multiple sensory perspectives. The ethical questions are substantial: concerns about bias and representation in generative models, about authorship and the value of human creation, and about students "cheating themselves" out of the creative struggle. Educators are responding by shifting the pedagogical emphasis from generation to critical editing and evaluation — teaching students to judge algorithmic output rather than merely produce it — and by developing critical AI literacy as a core arts competency.

Physical Skills: Embodied and Gestural Learning

Multimodal AI is beginning to address physical skills — the domain where text-based AI was most obviously inadequate. Systems that can observe movement, posture, and gesture can provide feedback on technique in sports, dance, instrument playing, and manual skills, and can support rehabilitation and motor learning. This is the least mature area, and the evidence base is thin, but the direction is clear: AI is moving from the symbolic domain of language to the embodied domain of action. The infrastructural demands are significant — cameras, microphones, and the privacy governance for continuous audiovisual data — and the ethical stakes are higher, because embodied data is more sensitive than text.

Accessibility and Equity of Multimodal Tools

Multimodal AI carries a distinctive equity risk. Text-based AI could reach students through devices they already owned; multimodal AI requires cameras, microphones, and bandwidth that many classrooms and homes lack, and the students without them will be excluded from the most advanced capabilities. The same technology that enables a vision-enabled tutor to see a student's handwritten work also requires the infrastructure to capture that work. Institutions should therefore treat multimodal deployment as an equity decision: which students get access, under what conditions, and with what alternatives for those who do not. There is also an accessibility opportunity: multimodal interfaces can serve learners who struggle with text — young children, students with reading disabilities, and learners in oral cultures — and the design of multimodal tools should be evaluated for whether it widens or narrows the participation gap.

The Common Challenges

Across all four domains, the same challenges recur. Privacy: multimodal systems collect voice and video data, which are more sensitive than text and require stricter governance under FERPA and comparable frameworks. Infrastructure: multimodal AI requires hardware — cameras, microphones, bandwidth — that text-based systems did not, raising equity questions about which students get access. Evaluation: the field needs model cards and evaluation frameworks that assess multimodal systems on pedagogical quality, not just technical capability. And pedagogy: the evidence so far suggests that multimodal AI is most effective as scaffolding for complex tasks, with students transitioning from generation to critical evaluation of algorithmic output. The institutions that engage with these technologies early will help define the pedagogical practices; those that wait may find the practices defined for them by platform developers.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)

  • Intelligent Avatar-Based Language Learning Systems: Linguistic, Paralinguistic and Affective Components (Frontiers in Computer Science) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Multimodal AI in Language Learning: Voice, Vision and Interaction (Computer Assisted Language Learning) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Exploring the Ethics of Multimodal Composition with AI: Student and Educator Perspectives (CITE Journal) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Multimodal Learning with AI: Capabilities, Infrastructure and Pedagogy (industry)

What roles are autonomous AI agents beginning to play in education — as persistent tutors, course designers, or student proxies — and what new questions do they raise?

Autonomous AI agents represent a qualitative shift from the chatbots that defined the first wave of generative AI in education. A chatbot responds to prompts; an agent pursues goals. Agents maintain state across extended sessions, use tools, follow strategies, and act over long horizons with minimal human intervention. As they enter education in three emerging roles — persistent tutors, course designers, and student proxies — they raise questions that the field has not yet answered.

Persistent Tutors

The most developed role is the persistent tutor. Unlike a chatbot that answers whatever it is asked, an agentic tutor maintains a model of the learner over time — mastery levels, misconceptions, learning styles — and uses that model to adapt instruction session after session. The technical architecture is typically dual-memory: long-term memory for stable student characteristics and working memory for session-specific context. Advanced systems use hierarchical multi-agent architectures, with specialized agents coordinating lesson planning, assessment generation, predictive risk detection, and domain-specific problem solving. The pedagogical design is also maturing: the best systems avoid providing direct answers, using Socratic questioning to promote critical thinking and metacognitive engagement, and are grounded in evidence-based practices such as active learning, cognitive load management, and growth-mindset promotion. Randomized controlled trials show that such systems can outperform in-class active learning on learning gains and engagement — but the same evidence base warns that over-automation can erode self-regulated learning if students become passive recipients of AI-generated content.

Course Designers

Agents are also entering the back end of education as course designers and operators. They can generate initial course frameworks by analyzing learning objectives and workforce demands, dramatically reducing the time educators spend building curricula. They automate administrative operations — scheduling, updating learning management system modules, managing attendance, checking accessibility — freeing instructors for mentorship and direct engagement. And they can analyze student progress across entire programs to adjust pacing and content dynamically, shifting education from a one-size-fits-all model toward individualized pathways. The governance question here is subtler than it appears: course design encodes values — what is worth learning, in what order, for whom — and delegating it to agents without explicit institutional oversight delegates those value decisions too. The emerging guidance is to start with narrow, measurable workflows and scale only after outcomes, educator review effort, and equity have been evaluated.

Student Proxies

The most novel and least understood role is the student proxy: agents that act on behalf of students — submitting work, negotiating deadlines, summarizing lectures, completing routine assignments, or representing a student's interests in interactions with institutional systems. Proxies raise the deepest questions because they blur the boundary between assistance and substitution. If an agent completes a student's assignment, what has the student learned? If an agent negotiates an extension, whose agency is being exercised? The assessment-integrity problem that chatbots created — students submitting AI-generated work — becomes structurally different when the AI is not a tool the student uses but an agent that acts for them. Institutions are only beginning to develop frameworks for distinguishing legitimate delegation (an agent that helps a student with a disability access the curriculum) from illegitimate substitution (an agent that does the learning for the student).

The Evidence Base and Its Gaps

The evidence on agentic AI in education is thinner than the enthusiasm suggests. The strongest findings come from tutoring systems that are agentic in limited senses — persistent, adaptive, memory-rich — and the randomized trials that show learning gains were conducted with substantial human oversight and careful pedagogical design. There is little evidence on the long-horizon autonomy that the term "agent" implies: systems that operate for weeks or months with minimal human intervention, making consequential decisions about what a student should learn next. The risks scale with autonomy — an agent that misjudges a student's readiness and adjusts the curriculum accordingly can do harm that a session-based tutor cannot — and the field has no established methods for evaluating such systems. The responsible trajectory is incremental: expand agent autonomy only as fast as the evidence and the oversight capacity allow, and treat every increase in autonomy as a new evaluation problem rather than a scaling of an existing one.

The New Questions

Across all three roles, the same questions recur. Accountability: when an agent makes a consequential error — a wrong placement, a harmful message, a failed escalation — who is responsible, and how is the agent's reasoning inspectable? Transparency: students, parents, and teachers need to know when they are interacting with an agent and what it is doing on their behalf. Agency: does the agent expand the student's agency or replace it? The principle of human-in-the-loop — educators retaining final authority over grading, discipline, and consequential decisions — is the current consensus answer, but it is a governance principle, not a resolution of the underlying questions. The transition to "autopilot" modes in education is underway, and the institutions that will navigate it successfully are those that treat agentic AI as a new kind of actor in the educational system — one whose capabilities, limits, and accountabilities must be designed, not assumed.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)
  • Memory-Rich and Multi-Agent Tutoring Systems: Architectures and Pedagogical Design (arXiv) (academic) [high credibility]
  • AI Tutoring Outperforms In-Class Active Learning: A Randomized Controlled Trial (Scientific Reports) (academic) [high credibility]
  • Agentic AI in Higher Education: Capabilities, Governance and Implementation (Ohio State) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • Agentic AI in Education: Use Cases, Trends and Implementation Playbook (industry)
  • AI Agents in Education: Curriculum Design and Personalized Pathways (industry)

What is known and unknown about AI in early childhood education, where developmental stakes are highest?

Early childhood is where the stakes of AI in education are highest and the evidence is thinnest. The developmental science is clear that the first years of life are a period of extraordinary plasticity, when the foundations of language, social-emotional competence, self-regulation, and attachment are laid down through responsive interaction with caregivers. It is equally clear that the primary driver of healthy development is face-to-face, contingent, emotionally attuned interaction with adults and peers. AI enters this landscape with genuine potential and genuine risk, and the field must be honest about how much is known and how much is not.

What AI Is Doing in Early Childhood Settings

AI is already present in early childhood education in several forms: interactive educational games and apps, smart robots, voice assistants, and adaptive platforms that adjust content to a child's pace and style. For educators and administrators, AI offers real utility in streamlining routine tasks — data collection, report generation, classroom management — which can free adults for the high-quality interaction that matters most. For children, the claimed benefits include personalized learning, language support through translation and interactive conversation, assistance for children with disabilities, and the development of skills such as creative inquiry and perspective-taking through conversational AI. None of these claims is trivial, and some have preliminary support, but the evidence base is dominated by small studies, short time horizons, and vendor-funded research.

What Is Known

The strongest knowledge concerns the conditions under which AI is least harmful and most useful. Developmental experts converge on the principle that AI should support adults rather than replace them: tools that assist educators in reflecting on classroom challenges, drafting activities, and tracking development are on firmer ground than tools that substitute for adult interaction. There is also reasonable evidence that AI can provide tailored support for children with diverse needs — communication aids, adaptive content, and practice environments for social skills — where the alternative may be no support at all. And there is consensus on the risks: over-reliance on technology can displace the play, hands-on exploration, and human interaction that drive development; excessive screen time is associated with negative cognitive, social, and emotional outcomes; AI systems collect sensitive behavioral data on children too young to consent; and models may contain biases that reinforce stereotypes or disadvantage children from marginalized backgrounds.

What Is Unknown

The unknowns are larger. There is no longitudinal evidence on the developmental effects of sustained AI interaction in early childhood — no studies that follow children from preschool through the primary years with measured AI exposure. The interaction between AI use and developmental stage is unstudied: the same tool may affect a two-year-old and a six-year-old differently, and the field has no framework for matching AI features to developmental readiness. The effects of AI companions on attachment, social development, and the capacity for human relationship are unknown, and the recent proliferation of AI "friends" marketed to children makes this the most urgent unknown of all. The equity question is also open: AI may narrow gaps by providing high-quality learning support in under-resourced settings, or widen them by concentrating benefits among children with the most capable adult mediation. The American Academy of Pediatrics and other developmental bodies have cautioned against the potential for over-reliance on technology, but the caution is a warning, not a finding.

The Role of Parents and Caregivers

In early childhood, the most important mediator of AI is the adult. The developmental evidence is clear that children learn through interaction with responsive adults, and the question for AI is not whether it can replace that interaction — it cannot — but whether it can support it. AI tools that prompt parents and caregivers with conversation starters, activity ideas, and developmentally appropriate questions can strengthen the adult-child interaction that matters; tools that occupy the child while the adult is absent substitute for it. The design implication is that early childhood AI should be evaluated for its effect on adult-child interaction, not just on the child's isolated performance: does the tool bring the adult into the learning loop, or does it push the adult out? This is a measurable property, and it should be a central criterion in the evaluation of any early childhood AI product.

Principles for the Known-Unknown Gap

Given the asymmetry between stakes and evidence, the responsible posture is precautionary and developmental. "Safety from the start" should be the operating principle: AI in early childhood should be validated by developmental experts, used only to supplement rather than replace play-based learning and in-person instruction, and governed by strict privacy protections under COPPA and comparable frameworks. Age-appropriateness should be a formal evaluation criterion, not a marketing claim. Educators should be trained to evaluate tools for developmental fit and bias, and to maintain the primacy of human relationships. And the field should invest in the longitudinal research it lacks — prospective cohorts with measured AI exposure, broad developmental outcome batteries, and comparison conditions that include equivalent adult-mediated activity. The developmental stakes are highest precisely because the window is narrow and the effects, for good or ill, are likely to be durable. Acting on the current evidence means proceeding with tools that support adults, protecting children's data, and refusing to treat the absence of evidence of harm as evidence of safety.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)

  • Artificial Intelligence in Early Childhood Education: A Systematic Review (Computers and Education: AI) (academic) [high credibility]
  • AI in Early Childhood Education: Risks and Recommendations (Institute for Child Success) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • FAQ: AI and Early Childhood Development (Zero to Three) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • AI in Early Childhood Education: Applications and Considerations (Brightwheel) (industry)

How is AI reshaping special education and individualized support plans in practice, beyond its general accessibility promise?

The general promise of AI for accessibility — text-to-speech, speech-to-text, captioning, translation — is well known. The more consequential transformation is happening in the operational core of special education: the Individualized Education Program (IEP), the legally mandated document that governs services for students with disabilities. AI is changing how IEPs are written, how progress is tracked, how assistive technology functions, and how educators allocate their time — and with those changes come new obligations and new risks.

The IEP as the Point of Transformation

IEPs are among the most time-intensive documents in education, and they are where AI is having its most concrete effect. Large language models are being used to draft IEP components: articulating SMART (Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound) goals from a student's current academic and functional levels, generating concise descriptions of present levels of performance, and drafting accommodations, modifications, and transition plans. AI-supported systems automate progress tracking and generate meeting agendas, allowing educators to shift time from paperwork to direct student interaction. The efficiency gains are real and documented by practitioners. But the IEP is also a legal instrument with binding obligations, and the risks are correspondingly serious: AI-generated goals can be generic, can miss the individualization that the law requires, and can embed the biases of training data. The professional consensus is that AI should draft, and qualified educators must review, refine, and own the final document — a human-in-the-loop requirement that is not a courtesy but a legal necessity under IDEA and FERPA.

Assistive Technology Beyond the Generic

The accessibility promise becomes concrete in AI-powered assistive technology. Augmentative and alternative communication (AAC) systems now analyze speech patterns to help students with severe speech difficulties express themselves, reducing the frustration that accompanies communication failure. Adaptive learning platforms adjust content difficulty and presentation in real time, matching pace and preference for students with dyslexia, ADHD, and autism. AI-driven text-to-speech, speech-to-text, and real-time captioning provide access to curriculum materials for students with visual, auditory, and language-based challenges. And virtual and augmented reality environments, often AI-powered, allow students to practice social interactions and behavioral responses in low-stakes, simulated settings — a genuinely new capability for students who need rehearsal before real-world encounters. For students whose alternative was no support at all, these tools are not conveniences; they are access to education.

Data-Driven Individualization

Beyond discrete tools, AI is changing the granularity of individualization. Systems that continuously analyze a student's performance can detect patterns — which task types cause difficulty, which scaffolds help, when frustration rises — that human observation, however skilled, cannot capture at the same resolution. This enables a more dynamic form of support planning: adjustments made in response to evidence rather than at annual review. The promise is an IEP that is a living document rather than a yearly snapshot. The risk is that the data driving these adjustments is incomplete, biased, or misinterpreted — a student's behavior in a digital environment is not the whole student, and models trained on general populations may misread the learning profiles of students with disabilities, who are systematically underrepresented in training data.

Ethical and Legal Constraints

Special education operates under the strictest data protections in schooling — FERPA and IDEA — and AI deployment must respect them. Student data must remain in secure, closed networks and must not be used to train public models. Algorithmic bias is a live concern: AI-generated content must be reviewed by qualified professionals, and the empathy, intuition, and relationship-building that are central to special education cannot be automated. There is also an equity dimension: differences in school funding and infrastructure mean that some students with disabilities will receive AI-enhanced support and others will not, and the digital divide in special education may be more consequential than in general education because the stakes for each student are higher.

Professional Development for the New Practice

Special educators are being asked to do something genuinely new: draft legally binding documents with AI assistance, evaluate AI-generated content for bias and individualization, and operate assistive technologies that did not exist when they trained. This requires professional development that is specific to special education — not generic AI literacy, but training in the IEP workflow, in the legal obligations that AI-assisted drafting must respect, and in the evaluation of AI output for the subtle errors that matter most for students with disabilities. The professional organizations in the field are beginning to develop this training, and institutions should treat it as a condition of deployment rather than an optional add-on. The stakes justify the investment: an AI-generated IEP that is efficient but generic is not a success, and the difference between efficiency and quality is precisely what professional judgment supplies.

The Practice Standard

What is emerging is a practice standard: AI as a drafting and analysis tool, human professionals as the authors of decisions, closed and protected data environments, and continuous professional development so that educators can evaluate AI output critically. The organizations representing special educators are clear that AI should be a pedagogical complement, not a replacement — and that the technology's value will be measured by whether it improves outcomes for the students who need the most support, not by the hours of paperwork it saves. The transformation is real, and it is happening now; the question is whether it will be governed by the field's values or by the technology's momentum.

  • The Next Frontier: AI in Special Education (Council for Exceptional Children) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • AI-Driven Assistive Technologies and Personalized Learning in Special Education (RCR Research Archive) (academic)
  • Combining AI and Special Education: A Comprehensive Guide (Kaltura) (industry)
  • How AI Tools Can Support Special Education Students and Teachers (EdTech Magazine) (industry)

How is AI transforming informal and out-of-school learning — self-teaching, homeschooling, communities of practice, and family learning?

Formal schooling has responded to AI with policies, committees, and cautious pilots. Informal learning has responded with adoption. In homes, hobby communities, and self-directed learning spaces, AI is being used at scale — often with less governance, less evidence, and more enthusiasm than in schools. The transformation of out-of-school learning is in some ways ahead of the institutional conversation, and it raises questions that formal education will eventually have to answer.

Self-Teaching and Interest-Led Inquiry

AI has changed what self-directed learning can be. The classic constraint on self-teaching was the limit of available expertise: a learner could only go as deep as their books, their network, and their own persistence allowed. AI removes that constraint. Learners can now explore complex subjects — quantum mechanics, philosophy, advanced mathematics — at their own pace, with an AI interlocutor that is non-judgmental, infinitely patient, and available at any hour. The pedagogical significance is that AI enables a genuinely interest-led form of inquiry: the learner asks the questions, and the AI responds at the learner's level, creating a Socratic loop that was previously available only to students with access to expert mentors. Tools that use Socratic-style guidance to help learners conceptualize projects and identify resources are explicitly designed to foster responsibility for one's own education. The risk is the mirror image of the promise: an AI that answers every question can also short-circuit the struggle through which understanding develops, and self-directed learners have no teacher to notice when the tool has become a substitute for thinking.

Homeschooling: The Administrative and Instructional Partner

Homeschooling families have been among the fastest adopters of AI, and the reasons are structural. Homeschooling parents carry the full administrative burden of education — lesson planning, curriculum design, progress tracking, and compliance documentation — and AI tools that generate lesson plans, unit overviews, and assessment activities in minutes convert hours of preparation into minutes, freeing parents for the mentorship and relationship-building that motivated homeschooling in the first place. Some families use specialized platforms to document activities and auto-complete state-mandated standards, simplifying portfolios and transcripts. For instruction, families use AI tutors that guide through problem-solving rather than providing answers, writing coaches that give real-time feedback, and creative tools that support artistic and narrative exploration. The homeschooling community's stance is notably different from formal education's: many advocates argue that banning AI is counterproductive, because AI literacy is a life skill their children will need regardless of institutional policy.

Communities of Practice

AI is also reshaping how informal learning communities function. Online communities of practice — hobbyists, language learners, makers, amateur scientists — have always been sites of peer learning, and AI is entering them in two ways. First, as a participant: AI systems that answer questions, review work, and provide feedback within communities, effectively adding an expert presence that small communities could not otherwise afford. Second, as a mediator: AI that helps communities organize knowledge, match learners with mentors, and curate resources. The transformation is double-edged: AI-mediated communities gain capability but risk losing the human texture — the encouragement, the shared struggle, the social identity — that makes communities of practice effective learning environments in the first place.

Family Learning

Finally, AI is changing family learning — the intergenerational learning that happens in households. Parents use AI to answer children's questions, to find learning resources, and to support their own learning as they help with homework. The technology can democratize the parent's role: a parent who did not study a subject can now support a child learning it, with AI providing the expertise the parent lacks. But family learning also concentrates the risks of informal AI use: children in homes with engaged, digitally literate parents gain a powerful learning partner, while children in homes without that mediation may encounter AI as an ungoverned entertainment or a source of misinformation. The equity question that formal education has struggled to answer is sharper in the informal sphere, where there is no institution to equalize access or oversight.

The Evidence Gap in Informal Learning

The transformation of informal learning is running far ahead of its evidence base. There is little research on whether AI-mediated self-teaching produces durable understanding, on how homeschooling families' use of AI affects children's learning trajectories, or on how AI-mediated communities of practice compare with human-mediated ones. The studies that exist are largely descriptive — documenting adoption and practice — rather than evaluative. This matters because informal learning is where the most consequential experiments are happening with the least oversight: children are using AI tutors at home in ways that no school would be permitted to deploy, and the outcomes are unmeasured. The research community should treat informal learning as a priority site for study, and families should be offered the same evidence-based guidance that schools are beginning to receive. The absence of evidence is not neutral; it is a decision by default about what the field chooses to know.

The Governance Gap

The defining feature of AI in informal learning is the absence of governance. There are no IEPs, no FERPA protections, no procurement standards, no professional development requirements in the informal sphere — only families, learners, and platforms. The evidence on outcomes is correspondingly thin, and the safety protections that formal deployments are beginning to require — age-appropriate design, data controls, human oversight — are largely absent from consumer tools. The transformation of informal learning is real and irreversible; the question is whether the governance frameworks that formal education is building will extend to the spaces where most learning increasingly happens, or whether informal learning will remain the unregulated frontier of AI in education.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)
  • Homeschoolers Embrace AI Even as Many Educators Keep It at Arm's Length (The 74) (industry)
  • Best AI Tools for Homeschooling: Tutoring, Writing and Administration (industry)
  • What Do New AI Tools Mean for Homeschooling? (Government Technology) (industry)
  • How Homeschooling Families Are Using AI (Fast Company) (industry)
  • How AI Can Transform Homeschools: A Parent's Guide to the Future of Learning (LittleLit) (industry)

What weak signals today — in research labs, startups, and experimental schools — most plausibly indicate what AI in education will look like in ten years?

Forecasting AI in education by extrapolating current headlines is a reliable way to be wrong. The more useful method is signal detection: identifying weak signals — early, low-amplitude indicators of change — in the places where the future is being built: research labs, startups, and experimental schools. These signals do not predict the future; they indicate where the plausible futures are being constructed, and they are worth attending to precisely because they are easy to dismiss.

Signals from Research Labs

The most important research signal is the shift from demonstration to hypothesis-driven evaluation. Research organizations are moving beyond asking whether AI works to asking under what conditions it works — which designs, which learners, which deployment models produce learning gains, and which produce harm. The AI Observatory and Action Lab's "Signal Libraries" exemplify this: systematic tracking of developments in research, news, and policy, designed to help education systems, particularly in low- and middle-income countries, avoid the mistakes of previous technology waves. The randomized controlled trials now emerging — AI tutoring against active-learning controls, supervised AI tutoring against human tutoring — are the same signal at the level of individual studies: the field is beginning to generate the evidence base that will determine which AI applications survive contact with reality. A second research signal is the growing attention to the conditions of harm: the self-regulation paradox, cognitive off-loading, and the persistence of learning deficits are being studied with the same seriousness as learning gains. Ten years from now, the AI applications that remain in education will be those that survived this evidence filter.

Signals from Startups

The startup signal is the shift from engagement metrics to unit economics. The first wave of edtech AI sold engagement; the emerging wave is being forced to sell outcomes, because education systems are beginning to demand evidence and because the economics of AI infrastructure are forcing discipline. The global demand for data-center capacity — estimated at trillions of dollars by 2030 — is driving up the cost of compute, and education systems are responding by demanding models that run on existing, low-cost hardware and by tying payments to verified learning gains. The startups that will matter in ten years are those building for this constraint: small, task-specific models rather than general-purpose giants; offline and low-bandwidth operation; and pricing tied to outcomes rather than seats. A related signal is the movement of AI talent: while AI PhD production is rising, an increasing share of graduates are remaining in academia, potentially strengthening the research capacity that will produce the next generation of educational AI.

Signals from Experimental Schools

In experimental schools and pioneering systems, the signal is the formalization of AI in the curriculum and the emergence of new pedagogical roles. Countries such as China and the United Arab Emirates have moved to mandate formal AI education, signaling a shift from elective exposure to core instruction. At the classroom level, the emerging roles are becoming visible: AI as tutor, providing one-on-one support where teachers are scarce; AI as co-professor, handling research, coding feedback, and assessment at the university level; and AI as companion, with a growing cohort of students turning to AI for social and emotional support — the last of these raising the most serious questions about dependency and the erosion of human relationship. The experimental-school signal is also visible in assessment: schools are beginning to redesign assessment to measure what students can do without AI, and to teach the critical evaluation of AI output as a core literacy. Ten years from now, the schools that experimented early will have defined the pedagogical practices that others adopt.

Signals from Policy and Regulation

A further signal is the formalization of governance itself. The EU AI Act's treatment of education as a high-risk domain, UNESCO's competency frameworks, and the OECD's guidelines for AI in education are not merely responses to the present; they are investments in a particular future, and their existence shapes which AI applications are built and deployed. Regulatory signals are weak in the sense that their effects are slow and indirect, but they are strong in the sense that they are durable: once an AI system is classified as high-risk in education, the classification does not change with the next product cycle. The direction of regulatory travel — toward transparency, human oversight, and evidence requirements — is itself a signal about the future, and it is one that startups and schools are already responding to. Ten years from now, the regulatory architecture being built today will be part of the environment in which all educational AI operates.

The Convergence

The weak signals converge on a plausible picture of AI in education a decade from now: a smaller set of evidence-backed applications, deployed with governance and measured against outcomes; AI embedded in the curriculum as a subject and in pedagogy as a tool; assessment redesigned around human capability with AI; and a sharpened equity divide between systems that can afford the infrastructure and those that cannot. None of this is certain — weak signals are weak precisely because they can be swamped by events. But they are the best evidence available about where the future is being built, and they are more reliable than the confident predictions that dominate the discourse. The institutions that will thrive are those that treat these signals as design inputs: building the evidence base, the governance, and the pedagogical practices that the signals indicate will matter.

Sources (edition 113, 5 sources)
  • AI Observatory and Action Lab: Signal Libraries for Education Systems (EdTech Hub) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • AI and the Future of Education: Disruptions, Dilemmas and Directions (UNESCO) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • AI Index Report 2026: Education Chapter (Stanford HAI) (institutional) [high credibility]
  • The Unit Economics of AI in Education: Infrastructure Costs and Outcome-Based Procurement (industry)
  • 25 Predictions About AI and EdTech (eSchool News) (industry)
